People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. 

Um, well, I hope I'm not wasting your time. It's just... I guess I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. I’ve always been a bit of a busy guy, which is usually a good thing, right? But it's like I just can’t really idle. It was fine before, but we have a new setup at my work—I do customer service—and it’s made it a lot harder to hide. 

I really want to do a good job and not be a distraction to my team or anything. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to come in.

It was fine before, but we have a new setup at my work—I do customer service—and it’s made it a lot harder to hide. \n\nI really want to do a good job and not be a distraction to my team or anything. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess maybe a little bit? I mean, I try really hard to look calm and collected, especially at work. Well, um... I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it actually goes back a long ways. Long before this job change, if I really think about it. 

I was a good kid, I promise! But... well, in grade school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright—which was always really nice to hear—but they'd also say I was a little, um, careless. And I guess I had a habit of talking out of turn. I think I just got a bit too eager to participate, you know? They did have to remind me to stay with my work every now and then. I always meant well, though, and I never wanted to be a disruption.

Oh, um... well, I guess like I mentioned, I did have that habit of talking out of turn back in school. I was just so eager to participate, you know? And, um... if I'm being completely honest, I suppose I still do something a little bit like that now at work.

Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? I mean, I really don't feel like I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I sit at my desk and I'm perfectly happy to be there, I promise. 

But, um... with the details, I do sometimes... well, I'll think I've completely finished a customer's ticket, and I feel good about it because I've helped them out. But then later, I might notice that I accidentally typed in the wrong account number, or maybe I just skipped over one of those little required boxes on the form. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. 

It's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. I really hope that doesn't sound too bad... does that make sense for what you're asking?

I really wouldn't want to make it sound like I've been struggling my whole life or anything! \n\nI mean, I've always tried really hard to keep on top of things and just be a reliable worker and a good husband. There were definitely long stretches where I think I managed just fine, or at least, I really hope I did. I never want to be a burden on anyone. Maybe I was a little scattered here and there, but I always tried to keep it under control so it wouldn't bother the people around me. \n\nSo, um, I wouldn't really say it's been a constant, everyday issue from childhood straight through to now. Does that help at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's causing, you know, terrible problems or anything. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main difficulty is with paying attention to details or with feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... I really think it's mostly just a work thing, to be honest. I don't want you to think I'm causing trouble everywhere I go! 

At home, things are pretty quiet. My wife handles a lot of the organizing, bless her heart, so I just follow her lead and try to be helpful where I can. I mean, maybe I'll forget to take the trash out exactly on time, or I'll leave a cabinet door open, but she's very patient with me. And with friends... well, we just chat and watch the game, so there aren't really any forms to fill out or details to mess up, you know? 

It's really just at the office where those little slips happen, mostly because we have so many different computer screens and steps to follow for every single call. I try to keep it all straight, I really do. So, um, I wouldn't say it's everywhere... just when things get a bit complicated at work. Is that okay? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.

Oh, gosh... impulsivity. Well, um, I certainly don't do anything dangerous or reckless! I really try to be mindful of my actions. 

But, um... I suppose there is one little thing, if I'm being completely honest. I just like to be helpful, you know? So sometimes, if I'm at my desk and I overhear my coworkers nearby working through a tricky customer problem... I sort of just jump right in with the answer. And, well, sometimes I do it before they've even finished talking, or before anyone actually asked me for my input. 

I really, really don't mean to be rude or step on anyone's toes! I just know the solution and I get so eager to help them fix it quickly for the customer. I've been trying very hard lately to just bite my tongue and wait unless they ask. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a nuisance or a know-it-all to the rest of the team... is that the sort of acting without thinking you meant?

They mentioned that I've been leaving some tickets unfinished, or making little mistakes here and there. I feel terrible about it because I really am trying my best, and I know it slows the team down when they have to double-check things. I just... I want to be a good employee, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad worker. I'm really trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it actually goes back a long ways. Long before this job change, if I really think about it. \n\nI was a good kid, I promise! But... well, in grade school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright—which was always really nice to hear—but they'd also say I was a little, um, careless. And I guess I had a habit of talking out of turn. I think I just got a bit too eager to participate, you know? They did have to remind me to stay with my work every now and then. I always meant well, though, and I never wanted to be a disruption. \n\nIs it okay that I'm bringing up stuff from that far back? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before questions were finished when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess like I mentioned, I did have that habit of talking out of turn back in school. I was just so eager to participate, you know? And, um... if I'm being completely honest, I suppose I still do something a little bit like that now at work. \n\nI really, really don't mean to be rude—I want everyone to like working with me—but... well, if I overhear my coworkers working through a customer problem nearby, I have this habit of just sort of jumping in. I'll give them an answer before they've even finished talking, or, um, before anyone even asked me for help. \n\nI just get so focused on wanting to solve the problem for them and be a good team player. I really hope they know I'm just trying to be helpful and not trying to step on anyone's toes. I try to be mindful of it. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope I'm answering these right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these behaviors or tendencies at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... at home or socially? Well, I try really hard to be a good listener with my friends and family. I don't think I cause too many issues there. I really try my best to keep things peaceful and pleasant, you know? \n\nI hope that makes sense. They did have to remind me to stay with my work sometimes. I really didn't mean to be difficult for them, I promise! I just had a lot of energy and got a little distracted, I suppose. \n\nI hope I'm not over-explaining things. Is that the kind of detail you needed?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, from childhood into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't know if I'd say it's been completely consistent the whole time. I really wouldn't want to make it sound like I've been struggling my whole life or anything! \n\nI mean, I've always tried really hard to keep on top of things and just be a reliable worker and a good husband. There were definitely long stretches where I think I managed just fine, or at least, I really hope I did. I never want to be a burden on anyone. Maybe I was a little scattered here and there, but I always tried to keep it under control so it wouldn't bother the people around me. \n\nSo, um, I wouldn't really say it's been a constant, everyday issue from childhood straight through to now. Does that help at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's causing, you know, terrible problems or anything. I'm trying so hard to double-check everything now so I don't let them down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee. I really do care about my work. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. And, um... if I'm being completely honest, I suppose I still do something a little bit like that now at work. \n\nI really, really don't mean to be rude—I want everyone to like working with me—but... well, if I overhear my coworkers working through a customer problem nearby, I have this habit of just sort of jumping in. I'll give them an answer before they've even finished talking, or, um, before anyone even asked me for help. \n\nI just get so focused on wanting to solve the problem for them and be a good team player. I really hope they know I'm just trying to be helpful and not trying to step on anyone's toes. I try to be mindful of it. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope I'm answering these right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these behaviors or tendencies at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... at home or socially? Well, I try really hard to be a good listener with my friends and family. I want them to know I care about what they're saying. \n\nI guess... maybe sometimes I can be a little bit, um, eager to jump into a conversation at a dinner party or something? But I don't think it's a big problem or anything. I just get excited to share a story or agree with them. And at home... well, my wife is wonderful, and we get along great. I don't think I cause too many issues there. I really try my best to keep things peaceful and pleasant, you know? \n\nI hope that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or pay attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I think I might have touched on that just a little bit a moment ago? I hope it's alright if I repeat myself. But yes, looking back, this really was around long before my recent job change. \n\nLike I mentioned, in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright, which my parents were always happy about, but they did also say I was a little careless, and that I talked out of turn. They did have to remind me to stay with my work sometimes. I really didn't mean to be difficult for them, I promise! I just had a lot of energy and got a little distracted, I suppose. \n\nI hope I'm not over-explaining things. Is that the kind of detail you needed?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, from childhood into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't know if I'd say it's been completely consistent the whole time. I really wouldn't want to make it sound like I've been struggling my whole life or anything! \n\nI mean, I've always tried really hard to keep on top of things and just be a reliable worker and a good husband. There were definitely long stretches where I think I managed just fine, or at least, I really hope I did. I never want to be a burden on anyone. Maybe I was a little scattered here and there, but I always tried to keep it under control so it wouldn't bother the people around me. \n\nSo, um, I wouldn't really say it's been a constant, everyday issue from childhood straight through to now. I hope that's an okay answer? I just want to make sure I'm being accurate and not, you know, exaggerating anything to make it sound worse than it is. Does that help at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's causing, you know, terrible problems or anything. I really do try my best every day, and I get along so well with the customers. I always want to make sure they feel heard and taken care of. \n\nBut, um... I guess lately, it has been coming up a little bit at work. Just in my reviews. My supervisors have, well, they've pointed out that sometimes I leave a few tickets unfinished, or maybe make a small mistake here and there. I'm trying so hard to double-check everything now so I don't let them down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee. I really do care about my work. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main difficulty is with paying attention to details or with feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? I mean, I really don't feel like I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh... before I started school? That is quite a ways back! I'm fifty-seven now, so my memory of being four or five is just a little fuzzy. 

But, um... looking back, I suppose this sort of thing has been around long before this recent job change. I definitely wasn't a bad kid, I promise! I always wanted to please my teachers and be good. But... well, in grade school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright—which was very kind of them to say!—but they would also put down that I was a bit careless. And they mentioned that I sometimes talked out of turn, or that I had to be reminded to stay with my work. 

I just got a little too eager or distracted sometimes, I think. I really hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student or a troublemaker in class. I really did try my best! Does... does that help answer your question?

They said it kind of slows the team down a little, which I feel just awful about. I really don't want to make anyone else's job harder! I'm trying so hard to double-check everything now so I don't let them down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee. I really do care about my work. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main difficulty is with paying attention to details or with feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? I mean, I really don't feel like I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I sit at my desk and I'm perfectly happy to be there, I promise. \n\nBut, um... with the details, I do sometimes... well, I'll think I've completely finished a customer's ticket, and I feel good about it because I've helped them out. But then later, I might notice that I accidentally typed in the wrong account number, or maybe I just skipped over one of those little required boxes on the form. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. I really hope that doesn't sound too bad... does that make sense for what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention to details or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I really think it's mostly just a work thing, to be honest. I don't want you to think I'm causing trouble everywhere I go! \n\nAt home, things are pretty quiet. My wife handles a lot of the organizing, bless her heart, so I just follow her lead and try to be helpful where I can. I mean, maybe I'll forget to take the trash out exactly on time, or I'll leave a cabinet door open, but she's very patient with me. And with friends... well, we just chat and watch the game, so there aren't really any forms to fill out or details to mess up, you know? \n\nIt's really just at the office where those little slips happen, mostly because we have so many different computer screens and steps to follow for every single call. I try to keep it all straight, I really do. So, um, I wouldn't say it's everywhere... just when things get a bit complicated at work. Is that okay? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with impulsivity, such as acting without thinking, that have caused issues for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... impulsivity. Well, um, I certainly don't do anything dangerous or reckless! I really try to be mindful of my actions. \n\nBut, um... I suppose there is one little thing, if I'm being completely honest. I just like to be helpful, you know? So sometimes, if I'm at my desk and I overhear my coworkers nearby working through a tricky customer problem... I sort of just jump right in with the answer. And, well, sometimes I do it before they've even finished talking, or before anyone actually asked me for my input. \n\nI really, really don't mean to be rude or step on anyone's toes! I just know the solution and I get so eager to help them fix it quickly for the customer. I've been trying very hard lately to just bite my tongue and wait unless they ask. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a nuisance or a know-it-all to the rest of the team... is that the sort of acting without thinking you meant?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity ever caused you to lose a job, damage a relationship, or experience other significant negative consequences?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! Nothing like losing a job or... or ruining a marriage, no. I'm very lucky, my wife is wonderful and we get along just fine. And I've always tried my best to be a good employee, I really have. Customers usually like me, I think, because I do care about helping them.\n\nBut, um... well, I suppose I should mention... it has sort of come up a little bit at work lately. Just in my performance reviews, you know? My supervisor—who is very nice, by the way, very fair—has pointed out those little mistakes I mentioned before. The unfinished tickets, or the times I miss a detail. He says it kind of... slows the team down a bit when things have to be double-checked or sent back to me. \n\nI felt just terrible when he told me that, because I really am trying so hard to do a good job and be a team player. I don't want to be a burden to anyone. I'm working on being more careful, I promise. Does... does that count as a negative consequence? I hope it doesn't mean I'm a bad employee, I just get a little tangled up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity before you started school?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... before I started school? I'm fifty-seven now, so my memory of being four or five is just a little fuzzy. \n\nBut, um... looking back, I suppose this sort of thing has been around long before this recent job change. But then later, I might notice that I accidentally typed in the wrong account number, or maybe I just skipped over one of those little required boxes on the form. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. I mean, maybe I'll forget to take the trash out exactly on time, or I'll leave a cabinet door open, but she's very patient with me. Oh, um... well, I suppose it hasn't really been a terrible, lifelong struggle, no. Like I mentioned, I've always been able to get by and do good work for my employers. I really pride myself on being dependable!

They did have to remind me to stay with my work sometimes. I really didn't mean to be difficult for them, I promise! I just had a lot of energy and got a little distracted, I suppose. \n\nI hope I'm not over-explaining things. Is that the kind of detail you needed?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, from childhood into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't know if I'd say it's been completely consistent the whole time. I really wouldn't want to make it sound like I've been struggling my whole life or anything! \n\nI mean, I've always tried really hard to keep on top of things and just be a reliable worker and a good husband. There were definitely long stretches where I think I managed just fine, or at least, I really hope I did. I never want to be a burden on anyone. Maybe I was a little scattered here and there, but I always tried to keep it under control so it wouldn't bother the people around me. \n\nSo, um, I wouldn't really say it's been a constant, everyday issue from childhood straight through to now. I hope that's an okay answer? I just want to make sure I'm being accurate and not, you know, exaggerating anything to make it sound worse than it is. Does that help at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's causing, you know, terrible problems or anything. I really do try my best every day, and I get along so well with the customers. I always want to make sure they feel heard and taken care of. \n\nBut, um... I guess lately, it has been coming up a little bit at work. Just in my reviews. My supervisors have, well, they've pointed out that sometimes I leave a few tickets unfinished, or maybe make a small mistake here and there. They said it kind of slows the team down a little, which I feel just awful about. I really don't want to make anyone else's job harder! I'm trying so hard to double-check everything now so I don't let them down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee. I really do care about my work. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main difficulty is with paying attention to details or with feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. And, um... if I'm being completely honest, I suppose I still do something a little bit like that now at work. \n\nI really, really don't mean to be rude—I want everyone to like working with me—but... well, if I overhear my coworkers working through a customer problem nearby, I have this habit of just sort of jumping in. I'll give them an answer before they've even finished talking, or, um, before anyone even asked me for help. \n\nI just get so focused on wanting to solve the problem for them and be a good team player. I really hope they know I'm just trying to be helpful and not trying to step on anyone's toes. I try to be mindful of it. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope I'm answering these right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these behaviors or tendencies at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... at home or socially? Well, I try really hard to be a good listener with my friends and family. I want them to know I care about what they're saying. \n\nI guess... maybe sometimes I can be a little bit, um, eager to jump into a conversation at a dinner party or something? But I don't think it's a big problem or anything. I just get excited to share a story or agree with them. And at home... well, my wife is wonderful, and we get along great. I don't think I cause too many issues there. I really try my best to keep things peaceful and pleasant, you know? \n\nI hope that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or pay attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I think I might have touched on that just a little bit a moment ago? I hope it's alright if I repeat myself. But yes, looking back, this really was around long before my recent job change. \n\nLike I mentioned, in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright, which my parents were always happy about, but they did also say I was a little careless, and that I talked out of turn. They did have to remind me to stay with my work sometimes. I really didn't mean to be difficult for them, I promise! I just had a lot of energy and got a little distracted, I suppose. \n\nI hope I'm not over-explaining things. Is that the kind of detail you needed?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, from childhood into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't know if I'd say it's been completely consistent the whole time. I really wouldn't want to make it sound like I've been struggling my whole life or anything! \n\nI mean, I've always tried really hard to keep on top of things and just be a reliable worker and a good husband. There were definitely long stretches where I think I managed just fine, or at least, I really hope I did. I never want to be a burden on anyone. Maybe I was a little scattered here and there, but I always tried to keep it under control so it wouldn't bother the people around me. \n\nSo, um, I wouldn't really say it's been a constant, everyday issue from childhood straight through to now. I hope that's an okay answer? I just want to make sure I'm being accurate and not, you know, exaggerating anything to make it sound worse than it is. Does that help at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and restlessness are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's causing, you know, terrible problems or anything. I really do try my best every day, and I get along so well with the customers. I always want to make sure they feel heard and taken care of. \n\nBut, um... I guess lately, it has been coming up a little bit at work. Just in my reviews. My supervisors have, well, they've pointed out that sometimes I leave a few tickets unfinished, or maybe make a small mistake here and there. They said it kind of slows the team down a little, which I feel just awful about. I really don't want to make anyone else's job harder! I'm trying so hard to double-check everything now so I don't let them down. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee. I really do care about my work. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main difficulty is with paying attention to details or with feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I guess if I really had to pick, it might be the attention to details? I mean, I really don't feel like I'm bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I sit at my desk and I'm perfectly happy to be there, I promise. \n\nBut, um... with the details, I do sometimes... well, I'll think I've completely finished a customer's ticket, and I feel good about it because I've helped them out. But then later, I might notice that I accidentally typed in the wrong account number, or maybe I just skipped over one of those little required boxes on the form. Sometimes I'll miss just one tiny instruction, and then the whole ticket comes right back to me to fix. \n\nIt's just silly little mistakes, really. I always fix them right away! I just get a little ahead of myself because I'm eager to help the next person in line. I really hope that doesn't sound too bad... does that make sense for what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention to details or restlessness in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I really think it's mostly just a work thing, to be honest. I don't want you to think I'm causing trouble everywhere I go! \n\nAt home, things are pretty quiet. My wife handles a lot of the organizing, bless her heart, so I just follow her lead and try to be helpful where I can. I mean, maybe I'll forget to take the trash out exactly on time, or I'll leave a cabinet door open, but she's very patient with me. And with friends... well, we just chat and watch the game, so there aren't really any forms to fill out or details to mess up, you know? \n\nIt's really just at the office where those little slips happen, mostly because we have so many different computer screens and steps to follow for every single call. I try to keep it all straight, I really do. So, um, I wouldn't say it's everywhere... just when things get a bit complicated at work. Is that okay? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any problems with impulsivity, such as acting without thinking, that have caused issues for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... impulsivity. Well, um, I certainly don't do anything dangerous or reckless! I really try to be mindful of my actions. \n\nBut, um... I suppose there is one little thing, if I'm being completely honest. I just like to be helpful, you know? So sometimes, if I'm at my desk and I overhear my coworkers nearby working through a tricky customer problem... I sort of just jump right in with the answer. And, well, sometimes I do it before they've even finished talking, or before anyone actually asked me for my input. \n\nI really, really don't mean to be rude or step on anyone's toes! I just know the solution and I get so eager to help them fix it quickly for the customer. I've been trying very hard lately to just bite my tongue and wait unless they ask. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a nuisance or a know-it-all to the rest of the team... is that the sort of acting without thinking you meant?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity ever caused you to lose a job, damage a relationship, or experience other significant negative consequences?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! Nothing like losing a job or... or ruining a marriage, no. I'm very lucky, my wife is wonderful and we get along just fine. And I've always tried my best to be a good employee, I really have. Customers usually like me, I think, because I do care about helping them.\n\nBut, um... well, I suppose I should mention... it has sort of come up a little bit at work lately. Just in my performance reviews, you know? My supervisor—who is very nice, by the way, very fair—has pointed out those little mistakes I mentioned before. The unfinished tickets, or the times I miss a detail. He says it kind of... slows the team down a bit when things have to be double-checked or sent back to me. \n\nI felt just terrible when he told me that, because I really am trying so hard to do a good job and be a team player. I don't want to be a burden to anyone. I'm working on being more careful, I promise. Does... does that count as a negative consequence? I hope it doesn't mean I'm a bad employee, I just get a little tangled up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity before you started school?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... before I started school? That is quite a ways back! I'm fifty-seven now, so my memory of being four or five is just a little fuzzy. \n\nBut, um... looking back, I suppose this sort of thing has been around long before this recent job change. I definitely wasn't a bad kid, I promise! I always wanted to please my teachers and be good. But... well, in grade school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright—which was very kind of them to say!—but they would also put down that I was a bit careless. And they mentioned that I sometimes talked out of turn, or that I had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI just got a little too eager or distracted sometimes, I think. I really hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student or a troublemaker in class. I really did try my best! Does... does that help answer your question?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity consistently across most of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um, like I said, there were those little things when I was young, but it really hasn't been a constant, terrible problem my whole life! I've managed just fine for the most part, really I have. I've always held down a job and tried my very best.\n\nIt's just... well, it's really just been the last year and ten months or so that it's gotten a little harder to keep a handle on things. That was when my job changed, you see. They moved me into this new hybrid role where I'm doing the live chats and managing the CRM system at the same time. It's a lot of jumping back and forth, and that's when I really started noticing these little slip-ups more. I'm trying so hard to adapt to the new system, I really am. I hope it doesn't seem like I'm just making excuses... does that make sense, that it would just become more noticeable recently because of the job change?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity for most of your life, or have they been more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose it hasn't really been a terrible, lifelong struggle, no. Like I mentioned, I've always been able to get by and do good work for my employers. I really pride myself on being dependable! \n\nIt's just... well, it's really been about a year and ten months since it became noticeably harder to manage. That's exactly when they shifted my position into this new hybrid role, handling the live chats and the CRM system all at once. Before that, things were quite manageable, I promise. It's just this new setup that has me a little... well, a little flustered sometimes. I hope that's alright, that it's just been more recent like that? I really am doing my best to get the hang of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity in settings outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... outside of work and school? Well... no, not really. I mean, I like to think I'm pretty put-together at home and with my friends! I try very hard to be a good neighbor and keep my house tidy, and I'm always on time for church on Sundays. \n\nI don't think I have any real trouble outside of the office, I promise. I try to keep things very peaceful at home. It's really just that new CRM system and the live chats at work that have me a little turned around. I hope that's okay? I just wouldn't want you to think my whole life is in disarray, because it really isn't! I manage things quite well at home, I think. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were looking for?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
